each with a fully worked solution and a TikZ graph of the model. Each diagram plots the model against time with
the initial value and any evaluated/target points marked, the long-term asymptote shown dashed for bounded
(cooling, logistic, capped-growth) models, both curves with their crossover for comparison questions, and a
tangent for the rate-of-change questions.

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 01\quad\small\texttt{(el5-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 01 (el5-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A colony of bacteria is modelled by \( B = 200e^{0.05t} \), where \( B \) is the number of bacteria and \( t \) is the time in hours. \\[3pt]  (a) Write down the initial number of bacteria. \\[3pt]  (b) Predict the number of bacteria after 10 hours. Give your answer to the nearest whole number.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
B = 200e^{0.05 \times 0} = 200e^0 = 200
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^0 = 1 \), so the initial number of bacteria is 200.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 10 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
B = 200e^{0.05 \times 10} = 200e^{0.5} \approx 200 \times 1.6487 \approx 330
\end{gathered}\]
Use a calculator to evaluate \( e^{0.5} \). Round to the nearest whole number.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 200 bacteria \quad  (b) 330 bacteria
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 02\quad\small\texttt{(el5-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 02 (el5-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The mass \( M \) (grams) of a radioactive sample after \( t \) years is given by \( M = 500e^{-0.03t} \). \\[3pt]  (a) State the initial mass. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the mass remaining after 20 years. Give your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
M = 500e^0 = 500 \text{ grams}
\end{gathered}\]
The initial mass is 500 g.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 20 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
M = 500e^{-0.03 \times 20} = 500e^{-0.6} \approx 500 \times 0.5488 \approx 274 \text{ g}
\end{gathered}\]
Use a calculator to evaluate \( e^{-0.6} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 500 g \quad  (b) 274 g
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 03\quad\small\texttt{(el5-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 03 (el5-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{An oven is preheating. Its temperature \( T\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) after \( t \) minutes is given by \( T = 220 - 200e^{-0.1t} \). \\[3pt]  (a) What was the initial temperature of the oven? \\[3pt]  (b) What is the temperature after 8 minutes? Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 220 - 200e^0 = 220 - 200 = 20\,^\circ\!\text{C}
\end{gathered}\]
The oven starts at room temperature, 20\textdegree C.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 8 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 220 - 200e^{-0.8} \approx 220 - 200 \times 0.4493 \approx 220 - 89.9 \approx 130\,^\circ\!\text{C}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate \( e^{-0.8} \approx 0.4493 \) using a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  20\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  130\,^\circ\!\text{C} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 04\quad\small\texttt{(el5-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 04 (el5-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The value \( V \) (\pounds ) of a motorbike \( t \) years after purchase is modelled by \( V = 8000e^{-0.15t} \). \\[3pt]  (a) State the purchase price. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the value after 4 years. Give your answer to the nearest pound.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 8000e^0 = \pounds 8000
\end{gathered}\]
The initial purchase price is \pounds 8000.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 8000e^{-0.6} \approx 8000 \times 0.5488 \approx \pounds 4390
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate \( e^{-0.6} \approx 0.5488 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \pounds 8000 \quad  (b) \pounds 4390
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 05\quad\small\texttt{(el5-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 05 (el5-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A savings account earns compound interest. The amount \( A \) (\pounds ) after \( t \) years is modelled by \( A = 2500 \times 1.04^t \). \\[3pt]  (a) Write down the initial amount invested. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the amount in the account after 6 years. Give your answer to the nearest penny.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 2500 \times 1.04^0 = 2500 \times 1 = \pounds 2500
\end{gathered}\]
Any number to the power 0 is 1.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 2500 \times 1.04^6 \approx 2500 \times 1.26532 \approx \pounds 3163.30
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate \( 1.04^6 \approx 1.26532 \) using a calculator. Each factor of \( 1.04 \) represents one year of 4\% growth, so after six years the amount has multiplied by roughly 1.265.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \pounds 2500 \quad  (b) \pounds 3163.30
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 06\quad\small\texttt{(el5-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 06 (el5-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A drug concentration \( C \) (mg/litre) in a patient's bloodstream after \( t \) hours is modelled by \( C = 4e^{-0.2t} \). \\[3pt]  (a) State the initial concentration. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the concentration after 3 hours. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
C = 4e^0 = 4 \text{ mg/litre}
\end{gathered}\]
The initial concentration is 4 mg per litre of blood.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
C = 4e^{-0.6} \approx 4 \times 0.5488 \approx 2.20 \text{ mg/litre}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate \( e^{-0.6} \approx 0.5488 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 4 mg/litre \quad  (b) 2.20 mg/litre
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 07\quad\small\texttt{(el5-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 07 (el5-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A population of insects is modelled by \( P = 150e^{0.06t} \), where \( t \) is the time in days. \\[3pt]  Find the value of \( t \) when the population first reaches 500. Give your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set \( P = 500 \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
150e^{0.06t} = 500 \Rightarrow e^{0.06t} = \frac{500}{150} = \frac{10}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 150.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.06t = \ln\!\left(\frac{10}{3}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln(e^{0.06t}) = 0.06t \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln(10/3)}{0.06} \approx \frac{1.2040}{0.06} \approx 20.1 \text{ days}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  t \approx 20.1 \) days
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 08\quad\small\texttt{(el5-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 08 (el5-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The mass \( M \) grams of a substance decays according to \( M = 800e^{-0.04t} \), where \( t \) is in years. \\[3pt]  Find the time taken for the mass to reduce to 200 g. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set \( M = 200 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
800e^{-0.04t} = 200 \Rightarrow e^{-0.04t} = \frac{200}{800} = 0.25
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 800.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-0.04t = \ln(0.25) = -\ln 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( 0.25 = \frac{1}{4} \), so \( \ln(0.25) = -\ln 4 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln 4}{0.04} \approx \frac{1.3863}{0.04} \approx 34.7 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
The negatives cancel.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  t \approx 34.7 \) years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 09\quad\small\texttt{(el5-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 09 (el5-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A savings account is modelled by \( A = 1000 \times 1.025^t \), where \( A \) is the amount (\pounds ) and \( t \) is the number of years. \\[3pt]  Find the number of complete years required for the account to exceed \pounds 1500.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up the inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
1000 \times 1.025^t > 1500 \Rightarrow 1.025^t > 1.5
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 1000.

\textit{Step 2: Take \( \log \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t\log(1.025) > \log(1.5)
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( \log(1.025) > 0 \), the inequality direction stays the same.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t > \frac{\log(1.5)}{\log(1.025)} \approx \frac{0.17609}{0.01072} \approx 16.4
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 4: State the number of complete years.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = 17 \text{ complete years}
\end{gathered}\]
The first complete year for which \( t > 16.4 \) is \( t = 17 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 17 complete years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 10\quad\small\texttt{(el5-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 10 (el5-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A car depreciates in value according to \( V = 12000 \times 0.88^t \), where \( V \) is the value (\pounds ) and \( t \) is the time in years. \\[3pt]  Find the number of complete years before the car is worth less than \pounds 5000.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up the inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12000 \times 0.88^t < 5000 \Rightarrow 0.88^t < \frac{5000}{12000} = \frac{5}{12}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 12000.

\textit{Step 2: Take \( \log \) of both sides. Note: \( \log(0.88) < 0 \), so the inequality reverses.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t\log(0.88) < \log\!\left(\frac{5}{12}\right) \Rightarrow t > \frac{\log(5/12)}{\log(0.88)}
\end{gathered}\]
Dividing by a negative number reverses the inequality.

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t > \frac{\log(5/12)}{\log(0.88)} \approx \frac{-0.3802}{-0.05552} \approx 6.85
\end{gathered}\]
Both numerator and denominator are negative, so \( t > 6.85 \).

\textit{Step 4: State the answer.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = 7 \text{ complete years}
\end{gathered}\]
After 7 complete years the car is first worth less than \pounds 5000.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 7 complete years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 11\quad\small\texttt{(el5-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 11 (el5-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A drug concentration \( C \) mg/litre decays according to \( C = 8e^{-0.35t} \), where \( t \) is in hours. \\[3pt]  Find the time at which the concentration first falls below 1 mg/litre. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set \( C = 1 \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
8e^{-0.35t} = 1 \Rightarrow e^{-0.35t} = \frac{1}{8}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 8.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-0.35t = \ln\!\left(\frac{1}{8}\right) = -\ln 8
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln(1/8) = -\ln 8 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln 8}{0.35} \approx \frac{2.0794}{0.35} \approx 5.94 \text{ hours}
\end{gathered}\]
The negatives cancel; evaluate using a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  t \approx 5.94 \) hours
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 12\quad\small\texttt{(el5-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 12 (el5-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{An oven cools after being switched off. Its temperature \( T\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) after \( t \) minutes satisfies \( T = 180e^{-0.08t} + 20 \). \\[3pt]  Find the time for the oven to cool to 60\textdegree C. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set \( T = 60 \) and isolate the exponential.}
\[\begin{gathered}
180e^{-0.08t} + 20 = 60 \Rightarrow 180e^{-0.08t} = 40 \Rightarrow e^{-0.08t} = \frac{40}{180} = \frac{2}{9}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract 20, then divide by 180.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-0.08t = \ln\!\left(\frac{2}{9}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln(e^{-0.08t}) = -0.08t \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{-\ln(2/9)}{0.08} = \frac{\ln(9/2)}{0.08} \approx \frac{1.5041}{0.08} \approx 18.8 \text{ min}
\end{gathered}\]
\( -\ln(2/9) = \ln(9/2) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  t \approx 18.8 \) minutes
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 13\quad\small\texttt{(el5-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 13 (el5-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The number of views \( V \) of a viral video after \( t \) days is modelled by \( V = 500 \times 3^t \). \\[3pt]  Find the number of complete days before the video exceeds 1 000 000 views.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up the inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
500 \times 3^t > 1\,000\,000 \Rightarrow 3^t > 2000
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 500.

\textit{Step 2: Take \( \log \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t\log 3 > \log 2000
\end{gathered}\]
\( \log(3^t) = t\log 3 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t > \frac{\log 2000}{\log 3} \approx \frac{3.3010}{0.4771} \approx 6.92
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 4: State the number of complete days.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = 7 \text{ complete days}
\end{gathered}\]
The first complete day for which \( t > 6.92 \) is \( t = 7 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 7 complete days
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 14\quad\small\texttt{(el5-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 14 (el5-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A population \( P \) of rabbits is modelled by \( P = Ae^{kt} \). Initially there are 80 rabbits. After 5 years there are 200 rabbits. \\[3pt]  Find the values of \( A \) and \( k \). Give \( k \) to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute \( t = 0 \) to find \( A \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = Ae^0 = A = 80
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^0 = 1 \), so \( A = 80 \).

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( t = 5, P = 200 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
80e^{5k} = 200 \Rightarrow e^{5k} = \frac{200}{80} = 2.5
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 80.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( \ln \) and solve for \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
5k = \ln(2.5) \Rightarrow k = \frac{\ln(2.5)}{5} \approx \frac{0.9163}{5} \approx 0.183
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 5.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  A = 80 \), \(\displaystyle  k \approx 0.183 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 15\quad\small\texttt{(el5-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 15 (el5-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The mass \( M \) grams of a substance decays according to \( M = Ae^{kt} \). The initial mass is 600 g and after 10 years the mass is 350 g. \\[3pt]  Find \( A \) and \( k \). Give \( k \) to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use \( t = 0 \) to find \( A \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
M = Ae^0 = A = 600
\end{gathered}\]
The initial mass gives \( A \) directly.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( t = 10, M = 350 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
600e^{10k} = 350 \Rightarrow e^{10k} = \frac{350}{600} = \frac{7}{12}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 600.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( \ln \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
10k = \ln\!\left(\frac{7}{12}\right) \Rightarrow k = \frac{\ln(7/12)}{10} \approx \frac{-0.5390}{10} \approx -0.0539
\end{gathered}\]
The negative value of \( k \) confirms this is decay.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  A = 600 \), \(\displaystyle  k \approx -0.0539 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 16\quad\small\texttt{(el5-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 16 (el5-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A fungus grows according to \( F = F_0 e^{gt} \). Initially it covers 3 mm\textsuperscript{2}. After 8 hours it covers 18 mm\textsuperscript{2}. \\[3pt]  (a) Find \( F_0 \) and \( g \). Give \( g \) to 3 s.f. \\[3pt]  (b) Predict the area covered after 15 hours.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) At \( t = 0 \): \( F_0 = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
F_0 = 3 \text{ mm}^2
\end{gathered}\]
Initial area gives \( F_0 \) directly.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( t = 8, F = 18 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3e^{8g} = 18 \Rightarrow e^{8g} = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 3.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( \ln \) and solve for \( g \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
g = \frac{\ln 6}{8} \approx \frac{1.7918}{8} \approx 0.224
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 4: (b) Substitute \( t = 15 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
F = 3e^{0.224 \times 15} = 3e^{3.36} \approx 3 \times 28.79 \approx 86.4 \text{ mm}^2
\end{gathered}\]
Use the values found in part (a).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  F_0 = 3 \), \(\displaystyle  g \approx 0.224 \)\(\displaystyle  \quad  (b) \approx 86.4 \text{ mm}^2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 17\quad\small\texttt{(el5-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 17 (el5-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The number of users \( N \) of a new app after \( t \) months satisfies \( N = N_0 e^{kt} \). After 2 months there are 5000 users and after 6 months there are 25000 users. \\[3pt]  Find the values of \( N_0 \) and \( k \). Give your answers to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write two equations using the given data.}
\[\begin{gathered}
N_0 e^{2k} = 5000 \quad \text{and} \quad N_0 e^{6k} = 25000
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each data point into the model.

\textit{Step 2: Divide the second equation by the first to eliminate \( N_0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{N_0 e^{6k}}{N_0 e^{2k}} = \frac{25000}{5000} \Rightarrow e^{4k} = 5
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{6k}/e^{2k} = e^{4k} \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4k = \ln 5 \Rightarrow k = \frac{\ln 5}{4} \approx \frac{1.6094}{4} \approx 0.402
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( \ln \) then divide by 4.

\textit{Step 4: Substitute back to find \( N_0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
N_0 = \frac{5000}{e^{2 \times 0.402}} = \frac{5000}{e^{0.804}} \approx \frac{5000}{2.234} \approx 2240
\end{gathered}\]
Use the first equation with the found value of \( k \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  N_0 \approx 2240 \), \(\displaystyle  k \approx 0.402 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 18\quad\small\texttt{(el5-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 18 (el5-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A radioactive substance has a half-life of 15 years. Its decay is modelled by \( A = A_0 e^{kt} \). \\[3pt]  (a) Show that \( k = -\dfrac{\ln 2}{15} \). \\[3pt]  (b) Find the value of \( k \) to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) The half-life means that when \( t = 15 \), \( A = \frac{1}{2}A_0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A_0 e^{15k} = \frac{1}{2}A_0 \Rightarrow e^{15k} = \frac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by \( A_0 \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
15k = \ln\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = -\ln 2 \Rightarrow k = -\frac{\ln 2}{15}
\end{gathered}\]
\(\displaystyle This completes the proof. \checkmark\)

\textit{Step 3: (b) Evaluate numerically.}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = -\frac{\ln 2}{15} \approx -\frac{0.6931}{15} \approx -0.0462
\end{gathered}\]
Use a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (b) \(\displaystyle  k \approx -0.0462 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 19\quad\small\texttt{(el5-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 19 (el5-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A radioactive substance has a half-life of 20 years and an initial activity of 640 Bq. Its activity is modelled by \( A = A_0 e^{kt} \). \\[3pt]  (a) Find \( k \) to 3 s.f. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the activity after 30 years. \\[3pt]  (c) After how many years does the activity first fall below 50 Bq? Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Use the half-life condition: \( A = 320 \) when \( t = 20 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
640e^{20k} = 320 \Rightarrow e^{20k} = 0.5 \Rightarrow k = \frac{\ln(0.5)}{20} = -\frac{\ln 2}{20} \approx -0.0347
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln(0.5) = -\ln 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 30, A_0 = 640, k \approx -0.0347 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 640e^{-0.0347 \times 30} = 640e^{-1.041} \approx 640 \times 0.3536 \approx 226 \text{ Bq}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 3: (c) Set \( A = 50 \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
640e^{-0.0347t} = 50 \Rightarrow e^{-0.0347t} = \frac{50}{640} \approx 0.07813
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 640.

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( \ln \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln(50/640)}{-0.0347} = \frac{-2.5507}{-0.0347} \approx 73.5 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
Both numerator and denominator are negative; \( t > 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  k \approx -0.0347 \)\(\displaystyle  \quad  (b) \approx 226 \text{ Bq} \quad  (c) \approx 73.5 \text{ years}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 20\quad\small\texttt{(el5-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 20 (el5-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A substance has a half-life of 8 years. The initial activity is 1000 Bq. \\[3pt]  After how many years is the substance reduced to a quarter of its original activity?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise that a quarter of the original activity means two half-lives have passed.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1}{2} \times \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
After one half-life: $\frac{1}{2}$. After two half-lives: $\frac{1}{4}$.

\textit{Step 2: Calculate the time for two half-lives.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2 \times 8 = 16 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
Each half-life is 8 years.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 16 years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 21\quad\small\texttt{(el5-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 21 (el5-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The temperature \( T\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) of a room \( t \) minutes after the heating is turned on is modelled by \( T = 22 - 16e^{-0.05t} \). \\[3pt]  (a) State the initial temperature of the room. \\[3pt]  (b) Explain what happens to \( T \) as \( t \to \infty \). \\[3pt]  (c) Explain why this model has an upper limit and state what it is.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 22 - 16e^0 = 22 - 16 = 6\,^\circ\!\text{C}
\end{gathered}\]
The room starts at 6\textdegree C.

\textit{Step 2: (b) As \( t \to \infty \), \( e^{-0.05t} \to 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
T \to 22 - 16(0) = 22\,^\circ\!\text{C}
\end{gathered}\]
The temperature approaches 22\textdegree C but never quite reaches it.

\textit{Step 3: (c) Since \( e^{-0.05t} > 0 \) for all finite \( t \), the temperature never reaches 22\textdegree C.}
\[\begin{gathered}
T < 22\,^\circ\!\text{C} \text{ for all finite } t
\end{gathered}\]
The upper limit is 22\textdegree C. The model prevents the temperature ever exceeding this.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  6\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  T \to 22\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) \quad  (c) Upper limit is \(\displaystyle  22\,^\circ\!\text{C} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 22\quad\small\texttt{(el5-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 22 (el5-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The penguin population \( P \) of an island is modelled by \( P = 3000e^{0.02t} \), where \( t \) is the number of years after a survey. \\[3pt]  (a) State the initial population. \\[3pt]  (b) Explain why this model may not be appropriate for large values of \( t \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 3000e^0 = 3000
\end{gathered}\]
The initial population is 3000 penguins.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Evaluate for a large \( t \), e.g. \( t = 200 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 3000e^{0.02 \times 200} = 3000e^4 \approx 163\,794
\end{gathered}\]
After 200 years the model predicts over 163 000 penguins.

\textit{Step 3: Explain the limitation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{The model allows } P \to \infty \text{ but island resources are finite.}
\end{gathered}\]
In reality, food supply, predators and habitat would limit growth. The model ignores these factors and is unrealistic in the long term.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 3000 \quad  (b) The model allows unlimited growth, which is unrealistic --- food supply and habitat would limit the population in reality.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 23\quad\small\texttt{(el5-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 23 (el5-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A car's value (\pounds ) \( t \) years after purchase is modelled by \( V = 2000 + 11000e^{-0.2t} \). \\[3pt]  (a) State the purchase price. \\[3pt]  (b) Explain the significance of the value 2000 in the model. \\[3pt]  (c) After how many complete years is the car worth less than \pounds 4000? Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 2000 + 11000e^0 = 2000 + 11000 = \pounds 13000
\end{gathered}\]
The car was purchased for \pounds 13 000.

\textit{Step 2: (b) As \( t \to \infty \), \( e^{-0.2t} \to 0 \), so \( V \to 2000 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
V \to \pounds 2000
\end{gathered}\]
The 2000 represents the scrap/minimum value --- the car will never be worth less than \pounds 2000 according to this model.

\textit{Step 3: (c) Set \( V = 4000 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2000 + 11000e^{-0.2t} = 4000 \Rightarrow 11000e^{-0.2t} = 2000 \Rightarrow e^{-0.2t} = \frac{2}{11}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract 2000 then divide by 11000.

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( \ln \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-0.2t = \ln\!\left(\frac{2}{11}\right) \Rightarrow t = \frac{-\ln(2/11)}{0.2} = \frac{\ln(11/2)}{0.2} \approx \frac{1.7047}{0.2} \approx 8.52
\end{gathered}\]
Use a calculator; the car is worth less than \pounds 4000 after 9 complete years.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \pounds 13 000 \quad  (b) \pounds 2000 is the minimum value as \(\displaystyle  t \to \infty \) \quad  (c) After 9 complete years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 24\quad\small\texttt{(el5-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 24 (el5-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A forest fire spreads so that the burnt area \( H \) hectares after \( t \) hours satisfies \( H = 10e^{bt} \). \\[3pt]  (a) Interpret the value 10 in this model. \\[3pt]  (b) After 2 hours the burnt area is 40 hectares. Find the value of \( b \). \\[3pt]  (c) Find the area burned after 5 hours. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
H = 10e^0 = 10 \text{ hectares}
\end{gathered}\]
The value 10 is the initial area of the fire when first observed.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 2, H = 40 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
10e^{2b} = 40 \Rightarrow e^{2b} = 4 \Rightarrow 2b = \ln 4 \Rightarrow b = \frac{\ln 4}{2} \approx 0.693
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 10, apply ln, then divide by 2.

\textit{Step 3: (c) Substitute \( t = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
H = 10e^{0.693 \times 5} = 10e^{3.466} \approx 10 \times 32.0 \approx 320 \text{ hectares}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 10 hectares is the initial area \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  b \approx 0.693 \)\(\displaystyle  \quad  (c) \approx 320 \text{ hectares}\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]
\begin{scope}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 25\quad\small\texttt{(el5-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 25 (el5-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Describe one limitation of using the model \( H = 10e^{0.693t} \) to predict the area burned by a forest fire over a long period.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Consider what happens as \( t \) gets large.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{As } t \to \infty, \; H \to \infty
\end{gathered}\]
The model predicts an infinitely large burnt area.

\textit{Step 2: Identify the real-world constraint.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{In reality, the fire is bounded by the total area of the forest.}
\end{gathered}\]
Once all the forest is burned, the model is no longer valid. It also ignores firefighting efforts, rainfall, and natural firebreaks.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad The model predicts unlimited growth, but the fire is constrained by the finite size of the forest. Other real-world factors (e.g. rainfall, firefighting) are also ignored.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 26\quad\small\texttt{(el5-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 26 (el5-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Two populations of bacteria are modelled by \( B_1 = 400e^{0.03t} \) and \( B_2 = 100e^{0.08t} \), where \( t \) is in hours. \\[3pt]  (a) Which population is larger initially? \\[3pt]  (b) Find the time at which both populations are equal. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) At \( t = 0 \): \( B_1 = 400 \) and \( B_2 = 100 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
B_1(0) = 400 > B_2(0) = 100
\end{gathered}\]
\( B_1 \) has the larger initial population.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( B_1 = B_2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
400e^{0.03t} = 100e^{0.08t}
\end{gathered}\]
Find \( t \) when the populations are equal.

\textit{Step 3: Divide both sides by \( 100e^{0.03t} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4 = e^{0.05t}
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{0.08t}/e^{0.03t} = e^{0.05t} \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( \ln \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.05t = \ln 4 \Rightarrow t = \frac{\ln 4}{0.05} \approx \frac{1.3863}{0.05} \approx 27.7 \text{ hours}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  B_1 \) is larger initially \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  t \approx 27.7 \) hours
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 27\quad\small\texttt{(el5-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 27 (el5-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Two cars depreciate as follows: Car A: \( V = 20000e^{-0.1t} \); Car B: \( V = 15000e^{-0.06t} \), where \( t \) is in years. \\[3pt]  (a) Which car costs more initially? \\[3pt]  (b) After how many years are both cars worth the same amount? Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) At \( t = 0 \): Car A = \pounds 20 000, Car B = \pounds 15 000.}
\[\begin{gathered}
V_A(0) = \pounds 20\,000 > V_B(0) = \pounds 15\,000
\end{gathered}\]
Car A costs more initially.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( V_A = V_B \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
20000e^{-0.1t} = 15000e^{-0.06t}
\end{gathered}\]
Find when the values are equal.

\textit{Step 3: Divide both sides by \( 15000e^{-0.1t} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{20000}{15000} = e^{(-0.06+0.1)t} \Rightarrow \frac{4}{3} = e^{0.04t}
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{-0.06t}/e^{-0.1t} = e^{0.04t} \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( \ln \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.04t = \ln\!\left(\frac{4}{3}\right) \Rightarrow t = \frac{\ln(4/3)}{0.04} \approx \frac{0.2877}{0.04} \approx 7.19 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) Car A \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  t \approx 7.19 \) years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 28\quad\small\texttt{(el5-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 28 (el5-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A population of fish is modelled by \( P = 600e^{0.04t} \), where \( t \) is in years. \\[3pt]  (a) Find the rate of growth \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}t} \) in terms of \( t \). \\[3pt]  (b) Evaluate the rate of growth when \( t = 5 \). Give your answer to 3 s.f. \\[3pt]  (c) Show that the rate of growth is proportional to the population.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Differentiate \( P = 600e^{0.04t} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}t} = 0.04 \times 600e^{0.04t} = 24e^{0.04t}
\end{gathered}\]
For \( y = Ae^{kt} \), the gradient is \( kAe^{kt} \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigg|_{t=5} = 24e^{0.2} \approx 24 \times 1.2214 \approx 29.3 \text{ fish/year}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 3: (c) Show the ratio \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}t}{P} \) is constant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}t}{P} = \frac{24e^{0.04t}}{600e^{0.04t}} = \frac{24}{600} = 0.04
\end{gathered}\]
The ratio is the constant 0.04, so the rate of growth is proportional to \( P \)\(\displaystyle . \checkmark\)

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}t} = 24e^{0.04t} \)\(\displaystyle  \quad  (b) \approx 29.3 \text{ fish/year}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 29\quad\small\texttt{(el5-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 29 (el5-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A radioactive substance decays according to \( M = 250e^{-0.05t} \) grams, where \( t \) is in years. \\[3pt]  (a) Find the rate of decay \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}M}{\mathrm{d}t} \). \\[3pt]  (b) Find the rate of decay when \( t = 10 \). Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}M}{\mathrm{d}t} = -0.05 \times 250e^{-0.05t} = -12.5e^{-0.05t}
\end{gathered}\]
The negative sign confirms the mass is decreasing.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 10 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}M}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigg|_{t=10} = -12.5e^{-0.5} \approx -12.5 \times 0.6065 \approx -7.58 \text{ g/year}
\end{gathered}\]
The substance is losing about 7.58 g per year at \( t = 10 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}M}{\mathrm{d}t} = -12.5e^{-0.05t} \)\(\displaystyle  \quad  (b) \approx -7.58 \text{ g/year}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 30\quad\small\texttt{(el5-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 30 (el5-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{\pounds 500 is invested in an account paying 2.5\% compound interest per year. No further deposits or withdrawals are made. \\[3pt]  (a) Write a model for the amount \( A \) (\pounds ) in the account after \( t \) years. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the amount after 10 years. Give your answer to the nearest penny. \\[3pt]  (c) Find the number of complete years before the account holds more than \pounds 800.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Each year the amount is multiplied by 1.025.}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 500 \times 1.025^t
\end{gathered}\]
1.025 = 1 + 2.5\% interest rate.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 10 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 500 \times 1.025^{10} \approx 500 \times 1.2801 \approx \pounds 640.04
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 3: (c) Set up the inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
500 \times 1.025^t > 800 \Rightarrow 1.025^t > 1.6
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 500.

\textit{Step 4: Take logs and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t > \frac{\log 1.6}{\log 1.025} \approx \frac{0.20412}{0.01072} \approx 19.0 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
So after 20 complete years the account first exceeds \pounds 800.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  A = 500 \times 1.025^t \) \quad  (b) \pounds 640.04 \quad  (c) 20 complete years
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 31\quad\small\texttt{(el5-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 31 (el5-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{A patient is given a dose of medicine. The concentration \( C \) mg/litre in their bloodstream after \( t \) hours satisfies \( C = Ae^{kt} \). Initially the concentration is 5 mg/litre. After 4 hours it is 2 mg/litre. \\[3pt]  (a) Find \( A \) and \( k \). Give \( k \) to 3 s.f. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the concentration after 6 hours. Give your answer to 3 s.f. \\[3pt]  (c) The medicine is effective when \( C \geq 0.5 \) mg/litre. Find the length of time for which the medicine is effective.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) At \( t = 0 \): \( A = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Initial condition gives \( A \) directly.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( t = 4, C = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
5e^{4k} = 2 \Rightarrow e^{4k} = 0.4 \Rightarrow k = \frac{\ln(0.4)}{4} \approx \frac{-0.9163}{4} \approx -0.229
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 5, apply \( \ln \), divide by 4.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Substitute \( t = 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
C = 5e^{-0.229 \times 6} = 5e^{-1.375} \approx 5 \times 0.2527 \approx 1.26 \text{ mg/litre}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 4: (c) Set \( C = 0.5 \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
5e^{-0.229t} = 0.5 \Rightarrow e^{-0.229t} = 0.1 \Rightarrow -0.229t = \ln(0.1)
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 5, apply \( \ln \).

\textit{Step 5: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{-\ln(0.1)}{0.229} = \frac{\ln 10}{0.229} \approx \frac{2.3026}{0.229} \approx 10.1 \text{ hours}
\end{gathered}\]
The medicine is effective for approximately 10.1 hours.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  A = 5, \; k \approx -0.229 \)\(\displaystyle  \quad  (b) \approx 1.26 \text{ mg/litre} \quad  (c) \approx 10.1 \text{ hours}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 32\quad\small\texttt{(el5-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 32 (el5-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A radioactive isotope has a half-life of 25 years. A sample initially has an activity of 480 Bq. The activity is modelled by \( A = 480e^{kt} \). \\[3pt]  (a) Show that \( k = -\dfrac{\ln 2}{25} \). \\[3pt]  (b) Find the activity after 50 years. \\[3pt]  (c) Find the original activity if the activity after 10 years is 300 Bq. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) After one half-life (\( t = 25 \)), the activity is halved: \( A = 240 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
480e^{25k} = 240 \Rightarrow e^{25k} = \frac{1}{2} \Rightarrow 25k = -\ln 2 \Rightarrow k = -\frac{\ln 2}{25} \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
This proves the given expression for \( k \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 50 \) (two half-lives).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 480 \times \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2 = 480 \times \frac{1}{4} = 120 \text{ Bq}
\end{gathered}\]
After two half-lives, the activity is $\frac{1}{4}$ of the original.

\textit{Step 3: (c) Let the original activity be \( A_0 \). Substitute \( t = 10, A = 300 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A_0 e^{-\frac{\ln 2}{25} \times 10} = 300 \Rightarrow A_0 e^{-0.2773} = 300
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( k = -\ln 2 / 25 \approx -0.02773 \).

\textit{Step 4: Solve for \( A_0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A_0 = \frac{300}{e^{-0.2773}} = 300e^{0.2773} \approx 300 \times 1.3195 \approx 396 \text{ Bq}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by \( e^{-0.2773} \) or multiply by \( e^{0.2773} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (b) 120 Bq \quad  (c) \approx 396 Bq\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 33\quad\small\texttt{(el5-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 33 (el5-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A town's population \( P \) is modelled by \( P = 45000e^{0.012t} \), where \( t \) is the number of years after 2020. \\[3pt]  (a) Estimate the population in 2030. \\[3pt]  (b) Estimate the year in which the population first exceeds 60 000. \\[3pt]  (c) Give one reason why the model may not be reliable in the long term.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) In 2030, \( t = 10 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 45000e^{0.12} \approx 45000 \times 1.1275 \approx 50\,737
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( t = 10 \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( P = 60000 \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
45000e^{0.012t} = 60000 \Rightarrow e^{0.012t} = \frac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 45000.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( \ln \) and solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln(4/3)}{0.012} \approx \frac{0.2877}{0.012} \approx 24.0
\end{gathered}\]
\( t \approx 24 \) years after 2020 = year 2044.

\textit{Step 4: (c) State a limitation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Population growth depends on factors like migration, birth rate, and economic conditions.}
\end{gathered}\]
These factors are not constant and cannot be captured by a single exponential model over a long period.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (a) \approx 50\,737 \quad  (b) 2044 \quad  (c) e.g. Assumes a constant growth rate; ignores migration and changes in birth/death rates.\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 34\quad\small\texttt{(el5-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 34 (el5-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{The number of subscribers \( S \) to a YouTube channel after \( t \) weeks satisfies \( S = S_0 e^{kt} \). After 3 weeks there are 8000 subscribers and after 9 weeks there are 50000 subscribers. \\[3pt]  (a) Find \( S_0 \) and \( k \). Give \( k \) to 3 s.f. \\[3pt]  (b) Predict the number of subscribers after 15 weeks. \\[3pt]  (c) Describe one limitation of this model.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Write two equations.}
\[\begin{gathered}
S_0 e^{3k} = 8000 \quad \text{and} \quad S_0 e^{9k} = 50000
\end{gathered}\]
Using each data point in the model.

\textit{Step 2: Divide to eliminate \( S_0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{6k} = \frac{50000}{8000} = 6.25 \Rightarrow 6k = \ln(6.25) \Rightarrow k = \frac{\ln(6.25)}{6} \approx \frac{1.8326}{6} \approx 0.305
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{9k}/e^{3k} = e^{6k} \).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( S_0 \) from the first equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
S_0 = \frac{8000}{e^{3 \times 0.305}} = \frac{8000}{e^{0.916}} \approx \frac{8000}{2.499} \approx 3202
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( k \) into the first equation.

\textit{Step 4: (b) Substitute \( t = 15 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
S = 3202 \times e^{0.305 \times 15} = 3202 e^{4.575} \approx 3202 \times 97.1 \approx 311\,000
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 5: (c) State a limitation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{The model allows } S \to \infty \text{ but growth will slow as the channel becomes saturated.}
\end{gathered}\]
In reality subscriber growth slows as the potential audience is reached.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  S_0 \approx 3202, \; k \approx 0.305 \)\(\displaystyle  \quad  (b) \approx 311\,000 \text{ subscribers} \quad  (c) Unlimited growth is unrealistic; growth slows as the audience saturates.\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 35\quad\small\texttt{(el5-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Exponential Growth and Decay 35 (el5-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{A Newton's Law of Cooling model gives the temperature \( T\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) of a hot drink after \( t \) minutes as \( T = 65e^{-0.04t} + 20 \). \\[3pt]  (a) State the initial temperature. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the temperature after 10 minutes. Give your answer to 3 s.f. \\[3pt]  (c) Find the time at which the drink cools to 35\textdegree C. Give your answer to 3 s.f. \\[3pt]  (d) Explain what the value 20 represents and state the long-term behaviour of \( T \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 65e^0 + 20 = 65 + 20 = 85\,^\circ\!\text{C}
\end{gathered}\]
The drink starts at 85\textdegree C.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 10 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 65e^{-0.4} + 20 \approx 65 \times 0.6703 + 20 \approx 43.6 + 20 = 63.6\,^\circ\!\text{C}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate \( e^{-0.4} \approx 0.6703 \).

\textit{Step 3: (c) Set \( T = 35 \) and isolate the exponential.}
\[\begin{gathered}
65e^{-0.04t} + 20 = 35 \Rightarrow 65e^{-0.04t} = 15 \Rightarrow e^{-0.04t} = \frac{15}{65} = \frac{3}{13}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract 20 then divide by 65.

\textit{Step 4: Apply \( \ln \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-0.04t = \ln\!\left(\frac{3}{13}\right) \Rightarrow t = \frac{-\ln(3/13)}{0.04} = \frac{\ln(13/3)}{0.04} \approx \frac{1.4663}{0.04} \approx 36.7 \text{ min}
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate using a calculator.

\textit{Step 5: (d) As \( t \to \infty \), \( e^{-0.04t} \to 0 \) so \( T \to 20\,^\circ\!\text{C} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
T \to 20\,^\circ\!\text{C}
\end{gathered}\]
The value 20 represents the room temperature --- the drink cools towards the ambient temperature but never falls below it.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  85\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  63.6\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  t \approx 36.7 \) min \quad  (d) 20\textdegree C is room temperature; \(\displaystyle  T \to 20\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) as \(\displaystyle  t \to \infty \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 36\quad\small\texttt{(el5-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 36 (el5-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A colony of bacteria doubles every 4 hours. Starting with 200, find the population after 20 hours.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Number of doublings}
\[\begin{gathered}
20 \div 4 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
A doubling time of 4 hours means the population is multiplied by 2 every 4 hours. Divide the total time by the doubling time to count how many doublings occur in 20 hours.

\textit{Step 2: Calculate}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 200 \times 2^5 = 6400
\end{gathered}\]
Each doubling multiplies the previous population by 2, so 5 doublings multiply the starting value by \( 2^5 = 32 \). This is the discrete form of exponential growth \( P = P_0 \cdot 2^{t/T} \) with doubling time \( T = 4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 6400 bacteria
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 37\quad\small\texttt{(el5-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 37 (el5-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A radioactive substance has half-life 5 years. If the initial mass is 80 g, find the mass after 15 years.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Number of half-lives}
\[\begin{gathered}
15 \div 5 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
The half-life is the time for the mass to halve. Dividing the elapsed time by the half-life counts how many halvings occur --- here three.

\textit{Step 2: Calculate}
\[\begin{gathered}
m = 80 \times \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^3 = 10 \text{ g}
\end{gathered}\]
Each half-life multiplies the remaining mass by \( \tfrac{1}{2} \), so after three half-lives only \( \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^3 = \tfrac{1}{8} \) of the original mass remains.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 10 g
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 38\quad\small\texttt{(el5-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 38 (el5-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A population is modelled by \( P = 1000e^{0.05t} \). Find: (a) the initial population; (b) the population after 10 years.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) t = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 1000e^0 = 1000
\end{gathered}\]
At \( t = 0 \) the exponent is zero and \( e^0 = 1 \), so the coefficient 1000 is the initial population --- the value of \( P \) at the start of the model.

\textit{Step 2: (b) t = 10}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 1000e^{0.5} = 1000 \times 1.649 = 1649
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( t = 10 \) into the model so the exponent becomes \( 0.05 \times 10 = 0.5 \). The factor \( e^{0.5} \approx 1.649 \) is how much the population multiplies over 10 years, since 0.05 is the continuous growth rate per year.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 1000 (b) 1649
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 39\quad\small\texttt{(el5-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 39 (el5-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The mass of a substance decays according to \( m = 50e^{-0.1t} \). Find when the mass is 25 g.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
25 = 50e^{-0.1t} \implies e^{-0.1t} = 0.5
\end{gathered}\]
Setting \( m = 25 \) means half the original mass remains. Dividing by 50 isolates the exponential so we can apply \( \ln \) cleanly in the next step.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln 2}{0.1} = 6.93
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides: \( -0.1t = \ln(0.5) = -\ln 2 \), and the negatives cancel. We use \( \ln \) (not \( \log \)) because the base of the exponential is \( e \), and \( \ln \) is its inverse. This is in fact the half-life of the substance.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle t \approx 6.93\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 40\quad\small\texttt{(el5-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 40 (el5-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A car depreciates at 15\% per year. Its initial value is \( \pounds 20000 \). Find its value after 5 years.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Model}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 20000 \times 0.85^5 = 20000 \times 0.4437 = 8874
\end{gathered}\]
Losing 15\% per year means 85\% of the value carries over, so the multiplier is \( 0.85 \). Compounding for 5 years gives \( 0.85^5 \approx 0.4437 \), meaning the car retains about 44\% of its original value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \pounds 8874
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 41\quad\small\texttt{(el5-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 41 (el5-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{An investment grows at 3\% compound interest per year. How long until it doubles?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up}
\[\begin{gathered}
1.03^n = 2
\end{gathered}\]
"Doubles" means the amount becomes \( 2 \times \) the initial value, so after dividing by the initial amount the multiplier \( 1.03^n \) must equal 2.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
n = \frac{\ln 2}{\ln 1.03} = \frac{0.693}{0.0296} = 23.4
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides: \( n\ln 1.03 = \ln 2 \). We use \( \ln \) to bring the variable down from the exponent --- any consistent log base (\( \log_{10} \), \( \ln \), etc.) would work here. Since you need a whole year, round up to 24.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Approximately 24 years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 42\quad\small\texttt{(el5-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 42 (el5-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The temperature of a cooling body is \( T = 15 + 70e^{-0.03t} \). Find the initial temperature and the temperature after 30 minutes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: t = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 15 + 70 = 85^\circ
\end{gathered}\]
At \( t = 0 \), \( e^0 = 1 \), so \( T = 15 + 70 \). The 70 represents the initial excess above the ambient temperature of \( 15^\circ \)C --- the body starts at \( 85^\circ \)C.

\textit{Step 2: t = 30}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 15 + 70e^{-0.9} = 15 + 70(0.4066) = 43.5^\circ
\end{gathered}\]
The exponent \( -0.03 \times 30 = -0.9 \) is negative, confirming decay: the temperature above the ambient \( 15^\circ \)C shrinks. After 30 minutes only about 40.7\% of the original excess remains, so the body sits at \( 15^\circ + 28.5^\circ \approx 43.5^\circ \)C.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Initial: \(\displaystyle 85^\circ\)C; after 30 min: \(\displaystyle 43.5^\circ\)C
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 43\quad\small\texttt{(el5-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 43 (el5-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A population of 5000 increases at 2\% per year. Write a model and find the population after 20 years.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Model}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 5000 \times 1.02^{20} = 5000 \times 1.486 = 7430
\end{gathered}\]
A 2\% annual increase means each year the population is multiplied by \( 1.02 \) (the growth factor), so after \( t \) years \( P = 5000 \times 1.02^t \). Substituting \( t = 20 \) gives the compounded factor \( 1.02^{20} \approx 1.486 \), roughly a 49\% increase over 20 years.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  P = 5000 \times 1.02^t \); after 20 years: 7430
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 44\quad\small\texttt{(el5-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 44 (el5-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A substance decays so that \( m = m_0 e^{-kt} \). After 8 hours, 75\% remains. Find \( k \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.75 = e^{-8k} \implies -8k = \ln 0.75
\end{gathered}\]
"75\% remains" means \( m/m_0 = 0.75 \), so the exponential factor equals 0.75. Apply \( \ln \) --- the inverse of \( e \) --- to pull the exponent down to a linear equation in \( k \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = \frac{-\ln 0.75}{8} = \frac{0.2877}{8} = 0.0360
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( \ln 0.75 < 0 \), the two negatives cancel and \( k \) is positive --- even though it is a decay constant. The negative sign already sits in front of \( k \) in the model \( m = m_0 e^{-kt} \), so a positive \( k \) corresponds to decay.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  k \approx 0.0360 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 45\quad\small\texttt{(el5-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 45 (el5-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The number of fish in a lake is modelled by \( N = \frac{2000}{1 + 9e^{-0.5t}} \). Find: (a) N when t = 0; (b) N as t approaches infinity.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) t = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
N = \frac{2000}{1 + 9} = 200
\end{gathered}\]
At \( t = 0 \), \( e^0 = 1 \), so the denominator is \( 1 + 9 \times 1 = 10 \). This logistic model starts with 200 fish --- the initial population.

\textit{Step 2: (b) As t -> infinity}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{-0.5t} \to 0 \implies N \to \frac{2000}{1} = 2000
\end{gathered}\]
The decaying exponential \( e^{-0.5t} \) tends to 0 for large \( t \), so the denominator approaches 1 and \( N \) approaches the numerator 2000. This limiting value is the carrying capacity --- the maximum population the lake can support --- and the logistic model prevents unlimited growth, unlike a pure exponential model.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 200 (b) 2000
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 46\quad\small\texttt{(el5-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 46 (el5-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Carbon-14 has a half-life of 5730 years. A sample has 40\% of its original C-14. How old is it?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find k}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = \frac{\ln 2}{5730} = 0.000121
\end{gathered}\]
The standard half-life relation \( k = \dfrac{\ln 2}{t_{1/2}} \) comes from setting \( e^{-kt_{1/2}} = \tfrac{1}{2} \) and applying \( \ln \). A small \( k \) corresponds to a long half-life, so carbon-14 decays very slowly.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.4 = e^{-0.000121t} \implies t = \frac{-\ln 0.4}{0.000121} = 7573 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
Setting the remaining fraction to 0.4 and applying \( \ln \) brings the unknown \( t \) out of the exponent. The two negatives cancel because \( \ln 0.4 < 0 \) --- and the answer is positive, consistent with time elapsed since death.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Approximately 7573 years old
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 47\quad\small\texttt{(el5-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 47 (el5-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A drug is eliminated from the body so that the amount \( A \) mg after \( t \) hours is \( A = 400e^{-0.2t} \). Find when less than 50 mg remains.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
50 = 400e^{-0.2t} \implies e^{-0.2t} = 0.125
\end{gathered}\]
Set \( A = 50 \) and divide by the initial dose (400) to isolate the exponential. The fraction \( 0.125 = \tfrac{1}{8} \) tells you the drug has dropped to one-eighth of the original concentration.

\textit{Step 2: Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{-\ln 0.125}{0.2} = \frac{2.079}{0.2} = 10.4 \text{ hours}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( \ln \) to undo the \( e \): \( -0.2t = \ln(0.125) = -\ln 8 \). The negatives cancel, so \( t \) is positive. Equivalently this is three half-lives (since \( \tfrac{1}{8} = \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^3 \)), each lasting \( \ln 2 / 0.2 \approx 3.47 \) hours.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad After 10.4 hours
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 48\quad\small\texttt{(el5-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 48 (el5-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The value of a painting increases according to \( V = 500e^{0.08t} \). Find: (a) the value after 10 years; (b) when it reaches \( \pounds 2000 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) t = 10}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 500e^{0.8} = 500 \times 2.226 = 1113
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( t = 10 \) so the exponent is \( 0.08 \times 10 = 0.8 \). The positive exponent confirms growth: the painting more than doubles over 10 years, reflecting the continuous growth rate of 8\% per year.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
2000 = 500e^{0.08t} \implies e^{0.08t} = 4 \implies t = \frac{\ln 4}{0.08} = 17.3
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 500 to isolate the exponential, then apply \( \ln \) --- its purpose here is to bring the exponent down so we can solve linearly for \( t \). Since \( e^{0.08t} = 4 \), we have \( 0.08t = \ln 4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \pounds 1113 (b) 17.3 years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 49\quad\small\texttt{(el5-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 49 (el5-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A cup of tea cools from \( 90^\circ \)C in a room at \( 20^\circ \)C. After 5 minutes it is \( 70^\circ \)C. Find \( k \) in \( T = 20 + 70e^{-kt} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute T = 70, t = 5}
\[\begin{gathered}
70 = 20 + 70e^{-5k} \implies 50 = 70e^{-5k} \implies e^{-5k} = \frac{5}{7}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract the room temperature (20) first --- it is the ambient floor the tea cools towards, not part of the decaying excess. The remaining \( 70e^{-5k} \) represents the temperature above ambient at time \( t \); dividing by 70 isolates the exponential.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = \frac{-\ln(5/7)}{5} = \frac{0.3365}{5} = 0.0673
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides: \( -5k = \ln(5/7) \). Since \( 5/7 < 1 \), \( \ln(5/7) < 0 \), and the negatives cancel to give a positive \( k \). The constant \( k \) controls how fast the tea cools --- a larger \( k \) would mean a faster drop towards \( 20^\circ \)C.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  k \approx 0.0673 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 50\quad\small\texttt{(el5-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 50 (el5-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The spread of a rumour is modelled by \( N = 500(1 - e^{-0.1t}) \) where \( N \) is the number who have heard it after \( t \) hours. Find: (a) N after 5 hours; (b) when 400 people have heard it.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) t = 5}
\[\begin{gathered}
N = 500(1 - e^{-0.5}) = 500(1 - 0.6065) = 197
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( t = 5 \) so the exponent is \( -0.1 \times 5 = -0.5 \). The decaying \( e^{-0.1t} \) measures the fraction yet to hear; \( 1 - e^{-0.1t} \) measures the fraction who have heard. The model approaches the cap of 500 as \( t \to \infty \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) N = 400}
\[\begin{gathered}
400 = 500(1 - e^{-0.1t}) \implies e^{-0.1t} = 0.2 \implies t = \frac{\ln 5}{0.1} = 16.1
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 500 to get \( 1 - e^{-0.1t} = 0.8 \), then rearrange to \( e^{-0.1t} = 0.2 \). Apply \( \ln \): \( -0.1t = \ln 0.2 = -\ln 5 \). The two negatives cancel --- a pattern that recurs whenever you solve a decay equation for time.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 197 people (b) 16.1 hours
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 51\quad\small\texttt{(el5-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 51 (el5-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A machine loses 12\% of its value each year. After how many years is it worth less than a quarter of its original value?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Model}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.88^n < 0.25
\end{gathered}\]
Losing 12\% per year means \( 88\% \) carries over, so the annual multiplier is \( 0.88 \). After \( n \) years the machine is worth \( V_0 \times 0.88^n \); dividing through by \( V_0 \) leaves the condition that the multiplier falls below \( \tfrac{1}{4} \).

\textit{Step 2: Take logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
n > \frac{\ln 0.25}{\ln 0.88} = \frac{-1.386}{-0.1278} = 10.8
\end{gathered}\]
Taking \( \ln \) gives \( n\ln 0.88 < \ln 0.25 \). Dividing by \( \ln 0.88 \) reverses the inequality because \( \ln 0.88 < 0 \) --- a key pitfall in decay problems. Both \( \ln \) values are negative, so the ratio is positive, and the smallest whole year is 11.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad After 11 years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 52\quad\small\texttt{(el5-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 52 (el5-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{The pressure \( P \) at height \( h \) km is modelled by \( P = 101e^{-0.12h} \). Find the pressure at 10 km.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 101e^{-1.2} = 101 \times 0.3012 = 30.4 \text{ kPa}
\end{gathered}\]
At \( h = 10 \) km the exponent is \( -0.12 \times 10 = -1.2 \). The negative exponent means pressure decays with altitude; the factor \( e^{-1.2} \approx 0.30 \) tells you the air at 10 km is at roughly 30\% of sea-level pressure.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 30.4 kPa
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 53\quad\small\texttt{(el5-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 53 (el5-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The number of cases of a disease grows according to \( N = 50e^{0.15t} \). How long until there are 500 cases?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
500 = 50e^{0.15t} \implies e^{0.15t} = 10 \implies t = \frac{\ln 10}{0.15} = 15.4 \text{ days}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by the initial 50 cases so the exponential factor equals 10 --- a tenfold increase. Apply \( \ln \) to bring the time variable down from the exponent; we use \( \ln \) because the base of the model is \( e \). The positive exponent confirms growth, and \( t \approx 15.4 \) days gives the "tenfold time" of this outbreak.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 15.4 days
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 54\quad\small\texttt{(el5-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 54 (el5-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Show that if \( y = Ae^{kt} \), then \( \ln y \) is a linear function of \( t \). State the gradient and y-intercept.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln y = \ln A + kt
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( \ln \) to both sides and use \( \ln(ab) = \ln a + \ln b \) together with \( \ln(e^{kt}) = kt \). The result is linear in \( t \) with gradient \( k \) and \( y \)-intercept \( \ln A \). This is why plotting \( \ln y \) against \( t \) is so useful: a straight-line plot confirms exponential behaviour, and reading off the gradient gives the growth/decay constant \( k \) directly.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient = \(\displaystyle  k \), \(\displaystyle  y \)-intercept = \(\displaystyle  \ln A \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 55\quad\small\texttt{(el5-055)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 55 (el5-055)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A capacitor discharges: \( V = 12e^{-t/RC} \) where \( R = 1000 \), \( C = 0.001 \). Find \( V \) after 2 seconds.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{RC = 1}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 12e^{-2/1} = 12e^{-2} = 12 \times 0.1353 = 1.62 \text{ V}
\end{gathered}\]
First compute the time constant: \( RC = 1000 \times 0.001 = 1 \) second. After \( t = 2 \) seconds the exponent is \( -t/RC = -2 \), so the capacitor retains \( e^{-2} \approx 13.5\% \) of its initial voltage. The time constant \( RC \) controls how quickly the discharge happens --- after one \( RC \), about 37\% remains.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 1.62 V
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 56\quad\small\texttt{(el5-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 56 (el5-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A town has population 8000 growing at 1.5\% per year. In how many years will it exceed 10000?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
8000 \times 1.015^n = 10000 \implies 1.015^n = 1.25
\end{gathered}\]
A 1.5\% growth rate gives an annual multiplier of \( 1 + 0.015 = 1.015 \). Dividing both sides by 8000 leaves the condition that the multiplier reaches \( 10000/8000 = 1.25 \) --- a 25\% increase.

\textit{Step 2: Take logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
n = \frac{\ln 1.25}{\ln 1.015} = 15.0
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides to bring \( n \) down: \( n\ln 1.015 = \ln 1.25 \). Both \( \ln \) values are positive (each base \( > 1 \)), so \( n \) is positive. Population first exceeds 10 000 in the 15th year.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 15 years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 57\quad\small\texttt{(el5-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 57 (el5-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Two populations: \( P_1 = 3000e^{0.02t} \) and \( P_2 = 5000e^{-0.01t} \). When are they equal?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set equal}
\[\begin{gathered}
3000e^{0.02t} = 5000e^{-0.01t}
\end{gathered}\]
Population \( P_1 \) is growing (\( +0.02 \)) while \( P_2 \) is decaying (\( -0.01 \)), so they must cross at some future time. Setting them equal gives an equation we can solve for \( t \).

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{0.03t} = \frac{5}{3} \implies t = \frac{\ln(5/3)}{0.03} = 17.0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by \( 3000e^{-0.01t} \): the exponents combine as \( 0.02t - (-0.01t) = 0.03t \). Apply \( \ln \) to bring \( t \) out of the exponent. The positive \( 0.03 \) reflects the net growth rate of \( P_1 \) relative to \( P_2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle t \approx 17.0 years\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 58\quad\small\texttt{(el5-058)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 58 (el5-058)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A lake contains 100 kg of pollutant. Each year 20\% is removed naturally. How long until less than 10 kg remains?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Model}
\[\begin{gathered}
100 \times 0.8^n < 10 \implies 0.8^n < 0.1
\end{gathered}\]
Removing 20\% per year leaves \( 80\% \), so the annual multiplier is \( 0.8 \). Dividing by the initial 100 kg leaves the requirement that the multiplier falls below \( \tfrac{1}{10} \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
n > \frac{\ln 0.1}{\ln 0.8} = \frac{-2.303}{-0.2231} = 10.3
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides: \( n \ln 0.8 < \ln 0.1 \). Dividing by \( \ln 0.8 \) reverses the inequality because \( \ln 0.8 < 0 \). Both \( \ln \) values are negative so the ratio is positive --- the smallest whole year satisfying this is 11.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad After 11 years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 59\quad\small\texttt{(el5-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 59 (el5-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The temperature of a freezer item placed in a room is \( T = 22 - 40e^{-0.05t} \). Find when T = 10.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
10 = 22 - 40e^{-0.05t} \implies 40e^{-0.05t} = 12 \implies e^{-0.05t} = 0.3
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange to isolate the exponential. The 22 is the room temperature the item is warming towards, and the 40 is the initial "deficit" below room temperature; we are looking for the moment the deficit shrinks from 40 to 12.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{-\ln 0.3}{0.05} = \frac{1.204}{0.05} = 24.1 \text{ min}
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) to undo the \( e \): \( -0.05t = \ln 0.3 \). Since \( \ln 0.3 < 0 \), the negatives cancel --- the standard pattern when solving decay equations for time.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle t \approx 24.1 minutes\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 60\quad\small\texttt{(el5-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 60 (el5-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A savings account pays 5\% interest compounded annually. Find the annual equivalent rate if compounded continuously.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Continuous equivalent}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^r = 1.05 \implies r = \ln 1.05 = 0.04879
\end{gathered}\]
Annual compounding multiplies by \( 1.05 \) each year; continuous compounding multiplies by \( e^r \) per year. Setting these equal so the two schemes produce the same balance over a year gives \( e^r = 1.05 \), and applying \( \ln \) extracts the continuous rate. The continuous rate is slightly less than 5\% because continuous compounding "works harder" over the year.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle r \approx 4.88\% continuous rate\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 61\quad\small\texttt{(el5-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 61 (el5-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A rumour spreads so that after \( t \) hours, the fraction who know is \( f = 1 - e^{-0.3t} \). After how many hours do 90\% know?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.9 = 1 - e^{-0.3t} \implies e^{-0.3t} = 0.1 \implies t = \frac{\ln 10}{0.3} = 7.68
\end{gathered}\]
Set \( f = 0.9 \) and rearrange: \( e^{-0.3t} = 1 - 0.9 = 0.1 \) is the fraction who have NOT heard the rumour. Apply \( \ln \): \( -0.3t = \ln 0.1 = -\ln 10 \), and the negatives cancel. Notice how the model approaches but never reaches 100\% --- the exponential decays towards 0, so \( f \to 1 \) asymptotically.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle t \approx 7.68 hours\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 62\quad\small\texttt{(el5-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 62 (el5-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Light intensity decreases with depth: \( I = I_0 e^{-0.4d} \) where \( d \) is in metres. At what depth is the intensity 1\% of the surface value?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.01 = e^{-0.4d} \implies d = \frac{-\ln 0.01}{0.4} = \frac{4.605}{0.4} = 11.5 \text{ m}
\end{gathered}\]
Setting \( I/I_0 = 0.01 \) (1\% of surface intensity), apply \( \ln \) to bring the depth \( d \) down from the exponent: \( -0.4d = \ln 0.01 = -\ln 100 \). The two negatives cancel and \( d > 0 \) as expected --- light intensity drops with depth, which is exactly what the negative exponent \( -0.4d \) encodes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle d \approx 11.5 metres\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 63\quad\small\texttt{(el5-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 63 (el5-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The rate of decay of a substance is proportional to the amount present. Write the differential equation and its solution.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differential equation}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dm}{dt} = -km
\end{gathered}\]
"Rate of decay proportional to the amount present" translates directly into \( \dfrac{dm}{dt} \propto m \). The negative sign captures that the amount is decreasing, and \( k > 0 \) is the decay constant.

\textit{Step 2: Solution}
\[\begin{gathered}
m = m_0 e^{-kt}
\end{gathered}\]
Separating variables and integrating gives \( \ln m = -kt + C \); exponentiating recovers \( m = m_0 e^{-kt} \), where \( m_0 \) is the initial mass at \( t = 0 \). The natural exponential is the unique solution because differentiating \( e^{-kt} \) gives back \( -k e^{-kt} \) --- proportional to itself.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{dm}{dt} = -km \); solution \(\displaystyle  m = m_0 e^{-kt} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 64\quad\small\texttt{(el5-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 64 (el5-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{House prices increase by 6\% per year. A house costs \( \pounds 200000 \) now. What will it cost in 8 years?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Calculate}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 200000 \times 1.06^8 \approx 200000 \times 1.59385 \approx \pounds 318\,770
\end{gathered}\]
Each year multiplies the price by \( 1.06 \) (the growth factor for \( +6\% \)). After 8 years that factor compounds to \( 1.06^8 \approx 1.59385 \), so the price is roughly 59\% above today's value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \pounds 318,770 (to the nearest pound)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 65\quad\small\texttt{(el5-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 65 (el5-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The charge on a capacitor builds up: \( Q = Q_0(1 - e^{-t/RC}) \). If \( Q_0 = 100 \), \( RC = 5 \), find Q after 10 seconds.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
Q = 100(1 - e^{-2}) = 100(1 - 0.1353) = 86.5 \mu\text{C}
\end{gathered}\]
After \( t = 10 \) seconds the exponent is \( -t/RC = -10/5 = -2 \). The factor \( 1 - e^{-t/RC} \) measures the fraction of the maximum charge \( Q_0 \) that has built up: at \( t = 2RC \) it is about 86.5\%. Eventually \( Q \to Q_0 = 100 \) as \( t \to \infty \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  Q \approx 86.5 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}
