\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 66\quad\small\texttt{(el5-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 66 (el5-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Data suggests \( y = ab^x \). When \( x = 0, y = 3 \) and when \( x = 4, y = 48 \). Find \( a \) and \( b \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: x = 0 gives a}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 3
\end{gathered}\]
At \( x = 0 \), \( b^0 = 1 \) regardless of \( b \), so \( y = a \). The coefficient \( a \) is therefore the value of \( y \) at \( x = 0 \) --- the "initial value" of the model.

\textit{Step 2: x = 4}
\[\begin{gathered}
3b^4 = 48 \implies b^4 = 16 \implies b = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Substituting the second data point gives a single equation in \( b \). Dividing by 3 isolates \( b^4 \), and the fourth root of 16 is 2. The base \( b \) is the per-unit growth factor: \( b = 2 \) means \( y \) doubles each time \( x \) increases by 1.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  a = 3, \; b = 2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 67\quad\small\texttt{(el5-067)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 67 (el5-067)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A ball bounces to 80\% of its previous height. Dropped from 2 m, after how many bounces is it below 0.5 m?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Model}
\[\begin{gathered}
2 \times 0.8^n < 0.5 \implies 0.8^n < 0.25
\end{gathered}\]
Each bounce multiplies the height by \( 0.8 \), so after \( n \) bounces the height is \( 2 \times 0.8^n \). Dividing through by 2 (the drop height) leaves the requirement that the multiplier falls below \( \tfrac{1}{4} \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
n > \frac{\ln 0.25}{\ln 0.8} = \frac{-1.386}{-0.2231} = 6.21
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( \ln \) to both sides: \( n \ln 0.8 < \ln 0.25 \). The inequality flips when dividing by \( \ln 0.8 < 0 \). Both \( \ln \) values are negative so the answer is positive, and we round up to the next whole bounce: 7.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad After 7 bounces
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 68\quad\small\texttt{(el5-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 68 (el5-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Show that a quantity with constant percentage growth rate \( r\% \) per unit time can be written as \( Q = Q_0 e^{kt} \) where \( k = \ln(1 + r/100) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Start from discrete}
\[\begin{gathered}
Q = Q_0(1 + r/100)^t = Q_0 e^{t\ln(1+r/100)} = Q_0 e^{kt}
\end{gathered}\]
Use the identity \( a^t = e^{t\ln a} \) to rewrite the discrete compounding factor as a natural exponential. Matching exponents with \( Q_0 e^{kt} \) gives \( k = \ln(1 + r/100) \). This is why "percentage growth rate" \( r \) and "continuous rate" \( k \) are not identical --- \( k \) is slightly smaller than \( r/100 \) for positive growth.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown: \(\displaystyle  k = \ln(1 + r/100) \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 69\quad\small\texttt{(el5-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 69 (el5-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Bacteria grow from 100 to 300 in 6 hours. Assuming exponential growth \( N = 100e^{kt} \), find: (a) k; (b) the population after 10 hours.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Find k}
\[\begin{gathered}
300 = 100e^{6k} \implies k = \frac{\ln 3}{6} = 0.183
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 100 (the initial count) so the exponential equals 3 --- the population has tripled. Apply \( \ln \) to bring \( 6k \) down, then divide by 6. The positive \( k \) confirms growth.

\textit{Step 2: (b) t = 10}
\[\begin{gathered}
N = 100e^{1.83} = 100 \times 6.23 = 623
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( t = 10 \) so the exponent becomes \( 10k \approx 1.83 \). The factor \( e^{1.83} \approx 6.23 \) is how much the colony multiplies in 10 hours under the fitted growth rate.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  k \approx 0.183 \) (b) 623 bacteria
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Modelling Growth/Decay 70\quad\small\texttt{(el5-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Modelling Growth/Decay 70 (el5-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{A radioactive element has decay constant \( k = 0.005 \) per year. Find: (a) the half-life; (b) the time for 90\% to decay; (c) the percentage remaining after 100 years.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Half-life}
\[\begin{gathered}
t_{1/2} = \frac{\ln 2}{0.005} = 138.6 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
The half-life satisfies \( e^{-k t_{1/2}} = \tfrac{1}{2} \); applying \( \ln \) gives \( -k t_{1/2} = -\ln 2 \), so \( t_{1/2} = \ln 2 / k \). A small \( k \) means a long half-life --- this isotope is very long-lived.

\textit{Step 2: (b) 10\% remaining}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.1 = e^{-0.005t} \implies t = \frac{\ln 10}{0.005} = 460.5 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
"90\% decayed" means 10\% remains, so \( N/N_0 = 0.1 \). Apply \( \ln \): \( -0.005t = \ln 0.1 = -\ln 10 \), and the negatives cancel.

\textit{Step 3: (c) After 100 years}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{-0.5} = 0.6065 = 60.7\%
\end{gathered}\]
At \( t = 100 \), the exponent is \( -0.005 \times 100 = -0.5 \), so the surviving fraction is \( e^{-0.5} \approx 0.6065 \) or about 60.7\%. Less than one half-life has elapsed, so over half the substance is still present --- consistent with \( t_{1/2} \approx 139 \) years from part (a).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 138.6 years (b) 460.5 years (c) 60.7\%
\end{answerbox}
